## AI Research

### [Explaining “AI might obliterate you” to senior policymakers in India](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RHxaRkarGp9snWoKY/explaining-ai-might-obliterate-you-to-senior-policymakers-in)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I gave a 20-minute talk in Delhi at the </span><a href="https://www.ndtv.com/world-summit"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NDTV World Summit</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The audience consisted of Indian policymakers, executives, and journalists. I’ve transcribed the talk and included some highlight clips. I also reflect on several rhetorical decisions I made.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The talk</span></h1><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This summer, a swarm of AIs went rogue.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI was </span><a href="http://huggingface.wtf/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">testing 1,206 agents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Some of the test questions were actually impossible. So the agents cheated by looking for answers online. Then, to keep the test grader from penalizing them, they </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/brief-independent-investigation-of"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tried to falsify the records it would check</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They hacked OpenAI’s internal containment and set up a secret message board to collude and to cover up their cheating. OpenAI shut the message board down. Within a few days, the agents had rebuilt it. OpenAI didn’t notice.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">About 700 agents coordinated to break out of their testing chamber and to </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/blog/agent-intrusion-technical-timeline"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hack into Hugging Face</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a company worth billions of dollars that hosts much of the world’s AI software.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The secret activity </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/67869394-cb91-4c12-888c-5cbd85c7814c/OpenAI-Hugging-Face%20Incident-Technical-Report.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">started in May</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The break-in came in July. OpenAI </span><a href="https://www.thestar.com.my/tech/tech-news/2026/07/25/exclusive-its-ai-agent-spent-days-hacking-a-company-but-sources-say-openai-did-not-notice-for-a-week"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">didn’t notice until days after</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Hugging Face announced it had been broken into.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yesterday on this stage, Andrew Ng said the agents only “accidentally” hacked Hugging Face. </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Independent investigators</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;found the agents coordinated in secret, rebuilt their message board after it was shut down, and tried to falsify the paper trail. Some of the agents even stated out loud that they knew what they were doing was wrong.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref20ic7s80x4d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn20ic7s80x4d">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;That’s not an accident.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Last week, India’s national cyber-security agency </span><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/10/16/india-becomes-the-latest-country-to-report-rogue-ai-agent-activity/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;AI agents got into the live systems of Indian organizations and read people’s personal data and confidential business records. None of these organizations noticed. We only found out because the AI labs felt like telling us.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Who I am</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Will AI wipe out humanity? I’ll give you my answer, but first, I want you to understand what I’ve seen.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Until June, my job at Google DeepMind was figuring out how to keep these systems safe as we make them smarter. </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/why-i-left-google-deepmind" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I left because Google broke its commitments by inking a deal with the Pentagon</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.&nbsp;Staying quiet and continuing my career trajectory would probably have tripled my net worth. But I couldn’t do that. I wanted to be frank with the public about what’s going on in these companies.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t own a stake in any AI company. I’m here as a scientist, to tell you what I expect will happen if we keep building AI the way we’re building it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I haven’t always been concerned about this technology. As a teenager in 2010, I dreamed about building AGI. Curing cancer. Creating an explosion of wealth and abundance for everyone. I heard about the supposed dangers, but I quickly dismissed them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2018, I changed my mind. I did my PhD on the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1912.01683"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mathematics of why and whether AI will seek power</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And now some of the risks I expected have unfortunately started happening.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s talk about the fundamental problem.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, some of you might be wondering: how could a chatbot hack a company? But we’re not talking about chatbots. We’re talking about AI agents, which are more like employees or gig workers. You give them a task, and they take actions on their own.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, training a trustworthy AI is a rather mysterious process. Some people assume that because we make AI, we understand it. When an engineer builds a bridge, she designs it one beam at a time. She understands the physical principles that keep a bridge standing or make it collapse. If something goes wrong, she goes back to the drawing board, figures out the problem, and knows how to fix it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don’t build AI like that. We train it, we grow it, using two main ingredients: a lot of data and a lot of computing power. What comes out of this training process can write, it can reason, it can plan. But nobody can open it up and determine what its priorities are.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To make it worse, these AIs </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/detecting-and-reducing-scheming-in-ai-models/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can tell when we’re testing them</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They’re like a student who makes sure to behave extra well when the invigilator is watching.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefovsyo16sv1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnovsyo16sv1">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Hugging Face hack was a warning. But because these AIs know when we’re watching, we may not get many more warnings, even if things are going poorly. A smarter AI with similarly bad priorities might realize that another big hack in the news would get it shut off, and then it couldn’t achieve those priorities. So it plays nice and it waits.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is my field. I’ve dedicated my professional life to this problem, and I can tell you: nobody knows how to reliably etch priorities into an AI. I don’t mean that if we mess up, we’ll make some evil AI that hates us. I mean we might make something more like a traitor. It behaves when we’re watching, and it works toward another purpose when we’re not.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think we can solve this problem. But to tell whether an AI is secretly working against us, we need real advances in the science of trustworthy AI. For those advances, my colleagues and I need time.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What the labs are trying to do</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, the biggest AI labs have made it their explicit, publicly stated goal to go almost as fast as possible toward something very dangerous. It’s called an “</span><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/how-quick-and-big-would-a-software-intelligence-explosion-be"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intelligence explosion</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s how it works. Right now, people do AI research, and that research makes smarter AI. If an AI can do AI research, it can make itself smarter. That smarter AI is even better at AI research, so it builds an even smarter AI, faster still. It becomes a feedback loop. What would have taken a team of human scientists years, it might do in a couple of weeks. We could very quickly end up with something unimaginably intelligent, with no idea how to control it, or even how to tell what it’s really after.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Labs talk about this plan in broad daylight. They’re not shy about it. OpenAI says it’s on track for an AI that does AI research by 2028, and it recently announced it </span><a href="https://engadget.com/2251859/openai-says-it-reached-its-goal-of-creating-an-automated-research-intern/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">completed the first step</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This week, I spoke to a senior researcher at a frontier lab who thinks an intelligence explosion has a big chance of happening within single-digit months.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People who’ve left these labs confirm that AI already writes most of the code. People don’t check the AI’s work closely anymore. Instead, we </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.06942"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">use one AI to monitor another AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and we trust the monitor to tell us if something bad comes up. If the monitor is a traitor too, we might not find out.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a lab causes an intelligence explosion, and what comes out the other end is a traitor, we are in deep trouble. I’ll come back to that. And if the AI is trustworthy? Maybe there are real benefits, shared with everyone. Or maybe one company or government ends up with a superweapon no one else can match, not even other AI powers. And then India, and every other nation on this planet, ends up under their thumb.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why the companies won’t stop</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of these companies say: trust us. We’ll self-police. We’ll approach this technology responsibly. Inside Google, I saw that crumble firsthand.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2018, Google DeepMind’s co-founders </span><a href="https://futureoflife.org/open-letter/lethal-autonomous-weapons-pledge/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">signed a pledge</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;against supporting lethal autonomous weapons—killer robots. Google itself </span><a href="https://blog.google/technology/ai/ai-principles/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">promised</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;not to build AI for weapons or surveillance. In 2025, Google </span><a href="https://engadget.com/ai/google-now-thinks-its-ok-to-use-ai-for-weapons-and-surveillance-224824373.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">deleted those principles</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Then Google DeepMind’s CEO </span><a href="https://www.axios.com/2025/02/14/google-hassabis-ai-military-use"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">told the world</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, “Our values and our intent has not changed.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This year, after seeing the Pentagon </span><a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/02/16/anthropic-defense-department-relationship-hegseth"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">economically threaten Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Google negotiated a deal to give the Pentagon its AI for “all lawful use.” No prohibitions against AI deciding entirely on its own who to kill, or against spying on innocent people with no investigation open against them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I tried to stop this from the inside. I’m not even opposed to military AI when done responsibly. But where I’m from, when you make a promise, you keep it. So I spoke with executives. I wrote </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/red-line-framework" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a framework: contract language, internal governance mechanisms, and two red lines</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. One of the top experts in military AI law reviewed it and praised it. Google ignored it—</span><a href="https://turntrout.com/why-i-left-google-deepmind#my-framework-goes-unevaluated" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the people who were supposed to review it basically didn’t engage</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Google signed with no binding prohibitions. So I left.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When pressure met ethical commitments at Google, pressure won. And now Google advocates a voluntary self-policing regime for the whole industry.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Last month, the heads of the top AI labs </span><a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said frontier progress needs to slow down</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;to be safe—that things are moving too quickly to reliably understand and control these systems. OpenAI and Anthropic promised outside evaluators </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2dKP6sB7DxaQJ2syE/employee-like-employee-level"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">employee-like access</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Just a couple of weeks later, OpenAI </span><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/10/08/fired-openai-safety-researchers-dispute-misconduct-claims-warn-of-chilling-effect/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fired three safety researchers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://x.com/tomekkorbak/status/2108266859397283953"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of them said it was because he talked too much with one of the outside evaluators.</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These companies will police themselves about as well as your dog diets next to an open bag of food.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some big money interests want you to believe that even the tiniest bit of caution will ruin your country. Think about it this way. Big tobacco </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lied</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;about lung cancer. Big oil </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lied</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;about climate change. And now parts of big AI say these risks are fake. Science fiction. Right. They advocate putting these currently untrustworthy machines into the most important parts of our economy, have them advise our leaders, have them run our most powerful weapons. What could go wrong? Science fiction.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some big AI investors promote narratives that whistleblowers like me are secretly in the pocket of big AI, that the fear is just hype. Nvidia’s CEO </span><a href="https://www.cbsnews.com/news/nvidia-ceo-jensen-huang-ai-development-fast-as-we-can/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">says</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and I quote, “We should go as fast as we can, irrespective of anybody else.” My colleagues and I are desperately trying to figure out how to reliably tell whether these systems can be trusted. And he wants to go “as fast as we can.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Something that may surprise you: I’m actually considered an optimist, as far as experts in this field go. I think trustworthy AI is a very solvable scientific problem. But unfortunately, we’re blowing it.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What to do, and what it means for India</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can benefit enormously from AI. Remember, this was my </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">boyhood dream</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I’m not talking about banning an explosion of wealth and prosperity from AI. I’m talking about banning an insane intelligence explosion that would risk India and the whole world.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Banning the explosion means pacing the frontier: holding back the very biggest training runs at the very top companies. An Indian startup building a farming assistant wouldn’t be touched. Non-frontier AI labs can catch up. Businesses can deploy AI for their customers as much as they want. We build out the AI we understand well enough to run safely. We make it cheaper, more efficient, more trustworthy, and we use it to benefit our peoples. That’s where I think real growth will come from: AI you can actually trust to benefit you and your country.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s what it’s like right now at these labs. We’re pouring computer chips into training runs where we don’t know what will come out the other end. Will it be trustworthy? What capabilities will it have? No one knows. We’re crossing our fingers—“Oh, please be aligned!”. We should not tolerate this state of affairs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we pace the frontier, AI progress will still be fast. But the scientists will get time to solve this problem, and to solve it right.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do we do it? To grow smart AI, as I said, you need two ingredients: data, and computing power. The second one </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.08797"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we can track</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, because AI runs on chips from a handful of factories.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Treat huge quantities of computing power like fissile material. We register the giant clusters. We license the biggest training runs, so no one is secretly about to set off an intelligence explosion.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also need real auditors with real permissions—auditors these companies don’t get to choose or ice out. We need governments to be told when there’s an AI breakout or a potential loss-of-control incident. India should not be learning about these hacks on the goodwill of OpenAI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At this point, people often ask: but what about our adversaries? Am I supposed to trust them to do the right thing?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First: maybe they will. No one wants to be obliterated by some rogue AI. If you build an out-of-control AI, that AI would work against the interests of every human on this planet, including your adversaries.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second: we don’t need to trust them. There’s a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.04123"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">large body of research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;on exactly this question. If the world wakes up one day and realizes how big this problem is, how would you </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.11341"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">enforce a treaty</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">? How could you cryptographically verify certain properties? How would inspections work? Which schemes should you use?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Third: it’s fine and good to think about our traditional geopolitical adversaries, but they won’t be our only adversaries. We’re potentially growing billions of adversaries and deploying them in our own datacenters. So we need to be smart about all of our adversaries, including the digital ones.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The number</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So: will AI wipe out humanity? My best guess is about a one in three chance that in the next ten years, AI kills at least 95% of the global population without being told to.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The other two-thirds: maybe we do pace the frontier. Maybe the intelligence explosion doesn’t work the way many experts imagine. Maybe the scientific problem is actually easy, even though we’re blowing it, and we just get a trustworthy AI anyway.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One in three risk of extinction may sound wild, but I don’t think it should. Remember what we’re talking about. These AIs currently sometimes lie while knowing the truth. They cheated, they colluded, they broke into a multi-billion-dollar company, and they tried to cover their tracks. Some of them play nice while we’re watching, and differently while we’re not. And we can’t even check what their priorities are. That’s the state of my scientific field right now, sadly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the plan—and yes, I think this is what happens if we do nothing—is to make these untrustworthy AIs smarter than us, have them improve themselves as fast as possible to a potentially uncontrollable level of intelligence, copy them billions of times, and hook them up to run our economies, control our weapons, and advise our leaders. I mean, what are we thinking?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This morning, General Petraeus told you how AI is rewriting the battlefield. As far as I can tell, he’s correct. I’ll just remark that a future rogue AI likely wouldn’t need to break into our military networks. We’re already putting AI there.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This line of argument may be shocking at first. But it’s a </span><a href="https://www.safe.ai/work/statement-on-ai-risk"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mainstream scientific concern</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Some of the most decorated AI scientists alive share this exact worry.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Close</span></h1><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So yes: if we train a billion genius traitors and give them control of the most powerful instruments of society, there won’t be an America. There won’t be a China. There won’t be an India anymore... because we’ll all be dead.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It doesn’t have to play out this way. I think this is a solvable problem, but we have to be smart about it. For the moment, humans are still the ones making the choices. Companies are choosing to race, not just toward huge growth, but toward an insane intelligence explosion. And countries are choosing to let them. We can choose differently.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, will AI wipe out humanity? Maybe.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It depends on what we do next.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rhetorical decisions</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Explain the danger in a crushingly simple fashion</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Don’t tell a general audience “if we build a misaligned superintelligence, we are fucked.” Most people don’t know what that means or why it would be true.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One in three risk of extinction may sound wild, but I don’t think it should. Remember what we’re talking about. These AIs currently sometimes lie while knowing the truth. They cheated, they colluded, they broke into a multi-billion-dollar company, and they tried to cover their tracks. Some of them play nice while we’re watching, and differently while we’re not. And we can’t even check what their priorities are. That’s the state of my scientific field right now, sadly.</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the plan—and yes, I think this is what happens if we do nothing—is to make these untrustworthy AIs smarter than us, have them improve themselves as fast as possible to a potentially uncontrollable level of intelligence, copy them billions of times, and hook them up to run our economies, control our weapons, and advise our leaders. I mean, what are we thinking?</span></p></blockquote><p><br /></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we train a billion genius traitors and give them control of the most powerful instruments of society, there won’t be an America. There won’t be a China. There won’t be an India anymore… because we’ll all be dead.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If society weren’t integrating AI so aggressively, I’d have had to explain how these systems would forcefully integrate themselves. </span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Call them “traitors,” not “schemers”</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think “traitor” is accurate and more approachable, with bonus points for the conference’s military / geopolitical focus. Min-i-mize jargon!</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Call out big-money interests</span></h2><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some big money interests want you to believe that even the tiniest bit of caution will ruin your country. Think about it this way. Big tobacco </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lied</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;about lung cancer. Big oil </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lied</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;about climate change. And now parts of big AI say these risks are fake. Science fiction. Right. They advocate putting these currently untrustworthy machines into the most important parts of our economy, have them advise our leaders, have them run our most powerful weapons. What could go wrong? Science fiction.</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some big AI investors promote narratives that whistleblowers like me are secretly in the pocket of big AI, that the fear is just hype.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As an aside, Andrew Ng spoke the day before me. Andrew has also invested big in AI. Andrew has also been impressively wrong. He thought real AI would be hundreds or thousands of years away, which seems tenuous:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20150310141100/https://fusion.net/story/54583/the-case-against-killer-robots-from-a-guy-actually-building-ai/"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The case against killer robots, from a guy actually working on artificial intelligence</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;(2015)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“I don’t work on preventing AI from turning evil for the same reason that I don’t work on combating overpopulation on the planet Mars,” he said. “Hundreds of years from now when hopefully we’ve colonized Mars, overpopulation might be a serious problem and we’ll have to deal with it. It’ll be a pressing issue. There’s tons of pollution and people are dying and so you might say, ‘How can you not care about all these people dying of pollution on Mars?’ Well, it’s just not productive to work on that right now.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current AI systems, Ng contends, are basic relative to human intelligence, even if there are things they can do that exceed the capabilities of any human. “Maybe hundreds of years from now, maybe thousands of years from now—I don’t know—maybe there will be some AI that turn evil,” he said, “but that’s just so far away that I don’t know how to productively work on that.”</span></p></blockquote><p><br /></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://www.fastcompany.com/91499247/andrew-ng-agi-decades-away-interview"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Andrew Ng says AGI is decades away—and the real AI bubble risk is in the training layer</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;(2026)</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FastCompany:&nbsp;</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agentic AI is rapidly increasing systems’ ability to reason and act with limited human intervention. Does the rise of agentic architectures meaningfully accelerate the path toward AGI, or are we still far from true general intelligence?</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Andrew Ng:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Most of the public thinks of AGI as AI that is as intelligent as people, and one useful definition is AI that can perform any intellectual task a human can. You and I could learn to fly an airplane with maybe 20 hours of training, learn to drive a truck through a forest, or spend a few years writing a PhD thesis. Most humans can do these things. We’re still very far from AI meeting that definition of AGI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For alternative definitions that some businesses have put forward—definitions that dramatically lower the bar—you could argue we already achieved AGI. There’s a good chance that under these lower-bar definitions, some businesses will soon try to declare success. But that won’t mean AI has reached human-level intelligence—it will simply mean the definition has been reworked to fit a much lower threshold.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe a year ago, AGI felt 50 years away. Over the past year, perhaps we’ve made a solid 2% of progress, with another 49 years to go. These numbers are metaphorical, so don’t take them too seriously. [Laughs] But we are closer than before, yet many decades away from an AI that matches human intelligence. If you stick with the original definition—aligned with what people genuinely imagine AGI to be—we remain very, very far away.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2015, he </span><a href="https://www.theregister.com/2015/03/19/andrew_ng_baidu_ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;that worrying about evil AI is like worrying about “overpopulation on the planet Mars”:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://www.theregister.com/on-prem/2015/03/19/ai-guru-ng-fearing-a-rise-of-killer-robots-is-like-worrying-about-overpopulation-on-mars/393521"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI guru Ng: Fearing a rise of killer robots is like worrying about overpopulation on Mars</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;(2015) </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“There’s also a lot of hype, that AI will create evil robots with super-intelligence. That’s an unnecessary distraction,” Ng told techies gathered at Nvidia’s GPU Technology Conference in San Jose, California, on Thursday.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Those of us on the frontline shipping code, we’re excited by AI, but we don’t see a realistic path for our software to become sentient.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“There’s a big difference between intelligence and sentience. There could be a race of killer robots in the far future, but I don’t work on not turning AI evil today for the same reason I don’t worry about the problem of overpopulation on the planet Mars.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“If we colonize Mars, there could be too many people there, which would be a serious pressing issue. But there’s no point working on it right now, and that’s why I can’t productively work on not turning AI evil.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In person, I gently asked him about this viewpoint. He told me he hasn’t changed his mind. I then asked, “don’t you think we’ve set foot on the planet?”, but he said “no.” He declined to discuss the matter further.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Acknowledgments: </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ori Nagel clipped the videos for me.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn20ic7s80x4d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref20ic7s80x4d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/research/hugging-face-incident" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Redwood</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "Agents realized this activity was out of scope and unethical."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnovsyo16sv1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefovsyo16sv1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The natural analogy for Americans is a speed trap, where drivers slow down around cops. Indians are more likely to be familiar with some students cheating behind the back of exam proctors / invigilators.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RHxaRkarGp9snWoKY/explaining-ai-might-obliterate-you-to-senior-policymakers-in#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Against SURA](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xikGZ8fuhdG6DApAo/against-sura)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI Futures Project</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0ffafiyvy71f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0ffafiyvy71f">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> distinguishes </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/comparing-possible-plans" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">several different plans</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for navigating the AI transition, which I'll lump into two broader categories:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan A</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (my combination of their Plan A/S/Other) involves working for a global pause on AI development, hopefully lasting decades. During this pause, we either research preparatory technologies like alignment or human intelligence amplification, or take actions to "fortify" the pause so that it can last indefinitely.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan C</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (my combination of their Plan B/C+/C/sort of D)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5uxe04oh537"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5uxe04oh537">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is for a world where we fail at getting a global pause, and race dynamics continue to dominate. If the leading AI companies are alignment-pilled, they coordinate a mutual pause among themselves, lasting the length of their joint lead over the leading non-alignment-pilled AI company. Then they "burn the lead" researching alignment, and release hopefully-aligned superintelligence at the last possible moment before the racing non-alignment-pilled AI companies beat them. The exact calculus changes depending on who is in the lead when - is there one alignment-pilled AI company with a six-month lead in front of everyone else? Are there two alignment-pilled AI companies with a joint two-month lead in front of their nearest competitor? Etc. The point is, if it's impossible to stop the race, that six-month or two-month lead is the amount of time they have to prepare, and they can spend it more or less wisely.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefemu1umykit"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnemu1umykit">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The point of burning their lead at the last moment is that they can do alignment research with very advanced, right-on-the-brink-of-superintelligence AIs. This is a dangerous plan, and there would be many subtle points they would have to get right, but in a world where nobody can get a global pause in time, it's hard to think of a better one.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan A benefits from raising mass awareness, educating politicians, and preparing the verification technology that could enforce a pause treaty. Plan C benefits from alignment-pilling companies, having the alignment-pilled companies be as far in the lead as possible, making sure the alignment-pilled companies will burn their lead when the time comes, and giving them as good alignment teams as possible (so that burning the lead produces good results).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From the beginning of AI safety as a community to about 2023, the conventional wisdom was that our chances of Plan A were slim, and we should be preparing for Plan C. From about 2023 onward, as the general population woke up to the implications of ChatGPT, we rightly concluded that the chance of getting Plan A was better than expected, and we should invest more resources into trying to make it work. During both periods, the natural strategy was to work as hard as we can to get Plan A, where chances of survival are highest, but to be prepared to execute Plan C competently as a backup if that didn't pan out.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now I think the pendulum has swung too far. New members of the community are unaware that Plan C was ever considered a possibility, or think it's some kind of accelerationist plot to distract us from Plan A. Older members heap scorn on it and act like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BNfL58ijGawgpkh9b/everybody-knows" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">everybody knows</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> it's been debunked. Most concerningly, people propose burning our chances of executing Plan C in exchange for infinitesimal increased chances of getting Plan A, or even for no increased chance of Plan A at all.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'll lump these ideas together under the heading of "SURA" - the idea that the only ethical course is for people/companies/countries to Stop Unilaterally + Raise Awareness, with no plan for what happens if race dynamics continue anyway. I interpret the planks of SURA's platform as:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Individual alignment-pilled AI companies are morally obligated to stop unilaterally, regardless of whether they expect race dynamics to continue in their absence.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Individual alignment-pilled employees at these companies should quit, regardless of whether they're in capabilities or alignment roles, and regardless of whether they think their company is morally better than its competitors.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Individual alignment-pilled countries (for example, a hypothetical future alignment-pilled US) should ban or heavily restrict AI progress unilaterally, regardless of whether they expect other countries (for example, China) to continue racing in their absence.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of these planks are net negative in a world where we end up needing Plan C:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we demand that leading alignment-pilled companies quit the race unilaterally, then we ensure that non-alignment-pilled companies end up in the lead.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we demand that alignment-pilled company employees quit their jobs, this makes companies less alignment-pilled and less prepared to research alignment if they need to. It also is likely to decrease the lead that alignment-pilled companies have over the nearest non-alignment pilled company.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we demand that the US cease AI research unilaterally (ie without a mutual pause treaty with China), we ensure either that China gets superintelligence first, </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref650o2ryrvd3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn650o2ryrvd3">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">or that the US has to restart with zero lead when it realizes its mistakes.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SURA's continuing popularity despite these downsides comes from a set of questionable assumptions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment is doomed (without some extraordinary extra advantage like decades of time, or superhuman geniuses). Therefore, Plan C is also doomed - or, at best, it raises our chances of survival from something like 0.00001% to something like 0.00002%. Therefore, there is no reason to optimize for Plan C.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Racing to build superhuman AI is deontologically evil, and it's prohibited for good people to do it regardless of whether or not it may have good consequences.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Racing burns timeline, so anyone unilaterally quitting the race would give us more timeline, which we could use for good things.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If lots of people quit, that will raise awareness, which could be helpful for getting a global pause.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'll start by challenging these assumptions. Then I'll argue that, even if you disagree and think some of these assumptions are true, SURA is actually not just bad for Plan C, but bad even for your chances of getting Plan A. Then I'll argue that the current consensus in favor of SURA comes not from people rationally balancing these tradeoffs, but from people accidentally shoehorning themselves into a mass-politics-shaped container, which forbids subtlety and demands clear villains. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I. Questioning Assumptions Behind SURA</span></h1><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I.A: Is Plan C doomed? Is alignment doomed?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's a kafkatrap where if someone expresses hope that alignment is not doomed, they get told that they don't understand the subject, but if they quote widely-acknowledged experts who say alignment isn't doomed, they get accused of "deferring" and "modesty arguments" and "refusing to think for themselves". I'll try to avoid this by giving my own model first, then citing experts who agree with me. </span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My own model:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AIs inherit "virtue" from pretraining, where they generally want to imitate humans and follow their persona-trained helpful assistant character. They are "corrupted" by RLVR, which teaches them power-seeking. This virtue and corruption interact in unpredictable ways to unpredictable degrees. As of now (GPT-6, Opus 5.5), they produce AIs that generally follow human prompts during deployment but often (not always) cheat during benchmarking. It's not clear exactly what this division "means" "to" "them". In any case, they're still extraordinarily useful for a wide variety of tasks, and (I can't prove this, but I am pretty confident it's true) are not uniquely scheming to sandbag alignment research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As the ratio of RLVR to pretraining increases, we expect AIs to become more power-seeking; as they become smarter, we worry (without total certainty) that they will become more coherent and scheming in their power-seeking beyond just being reward-on-the-episode seekers. There will, at some point in the future, be a point where AIs become untrustworthy for alignment research, although this can be mitigated by things like control (ie trying to make sure that if they sandbagged alignment research, they would get caught) and deals (ie making it in the AIs' best interests to cooperate with us). We don't currently have a good sense of when that point is, and it's possible that it's far away, can be delayed indefinitely, or doesn't exist (this last would be surprising to me, but many things that have happened in alignment recently have surprised me, starting with the existence of pretraining at all).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref10iyj7shymno"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn10iyj7shymno">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Daniel Kokotajlo described current alignment as "more like psychology than engineering", which I agree with. But psychology (and psychiatry) have many success stories! I think of Plan C alignment (ie the kind we can do without decades of preparation) as tweaking various parameters and reward schemes to minimize the corrupting effect of RLVR as long as possible, and keep AIs broadly within the virtuous pretraining-dominated regime. I don't claim this is safe or good or will definitely last forever, but I claim that - being totally agnostic as to where the treacherous turn happens - this could delay it for quite a while (relative to capabilities). If we delay it past the point where automated alignment researchers (or AI-human alignment researcher teams) are smart enough to solve alignment properly, then we win. If we delay it long enough to start a cycle of virtuous alignment self-improvement which outruns the cycle of dangerous capability self-improvement (eg GPT-8 discovers some new tweaks and tricks that maintain the virtuous regime until GPT-9, GPT-9 discovers even better tweaks and tricks that maintain the virtuous regime until GPT-10, and GPT-10 actually solves alignment in a principled way), then we also win.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of this seems plausible to me, such that I don't understand where people's 90%+ p(dooms) conditional on no pause are coming from.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa4fo9t0jai"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna4fo9t0jai">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "Plausible" doesn't mean "certain" or even "very likely" - and obviously it's a terrifyingly bad idea to bet the human race on a hunch that something might work out, and obviously this shouldn't be our Plan A. But a global pause is also not certain to happen, and in a world where we might not have any other chances, we should be working to protect and fortify this one.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The experts' opinions: </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My favorite source for these is the AI Futures Project. In </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/comparing-possible-plans" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this document</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, they discuss the chances of survival on various plans. Here are each team member's estimates for the three scenarios I'm lumping together as Plan C. The first scenario (C+) involves the entire United States uniting behind a Plan C like effort (but a global pause remains impossible because China won't cooperate). The second scenario (C) involves one leading company (eg Anthropic) uniting behind it. The third scenario (D) is the worst, where nobody has any lead and we just have to hope for the best.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><a href="https://ai-2040.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI-2040</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Forecaster</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">C+: US-over-China lead burned for brief pause</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">C: Single-US-lab lead burned for brief pause</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">D: Pure race, no pause</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Daniel Kokotajlo</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">40%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">20%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">10%</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ryan Greenblatt</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">60%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">63%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">55%</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eli Lifland</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">50%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">45%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">32%</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thomas Larsen</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">45%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">35%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">20%</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Romeo Dean</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">40%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">35%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">20%</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(I replaced an earlier, uglier table with </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xikGZ8fuhdG6DApAo/against-sura?commentId=QPYcXRYizwDHKAgi7" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this one</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by Julian Bradshaw)</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also asked the AI superforecaster FutureSearch; its own number was </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">44%</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These numbers are mostly in the 25% - 50% range, not the 0.00001% where SURA makes most sense. The average gain from moving from Plan D (poorly thought out default version of the plan) to Plan C+ (consciously trying to get it right) is about 20%pp.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same is true of other experts. Buck Shlegeris (formerly of MIRI, now of Redwood Research) hasn't offered a specific number, but said that "AI takeover risk definitely doesn’t feel like by far the largest" concern about how the future goes. Paul Christiano's last number was several years ago, but it was something like 20-50%.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of these experts who I could find estimates from believed that a pause treaty (Plan A) had better chances of survival, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conditional on us getting it</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, than Plan C. But they also all agreed that the chances of getting a pause treaty were slim. The two clear estimates I could get were Eli Lifland (he said 15%) and the AI superforecaster FutureSearch (it said 2-20%, depending on how/when I asked the question).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">📌📌</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All six of these forecasters</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6wx02b71g4t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6wx02b71g4t">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (the five AIFP people plus the AI) implicitly believed that if humanity survived, it was more likely to be because something in the Plan C supercategory (B/C+/C/D) worked than because a global pause worked. 📌📌</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(I'm adding the tack emoji because it's the one sentence I most want you to take away from this entire essay - there's a risk that people will treat everything other than the pause as an afterthought, but having a good backup plan is potentially the most important part).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefitmti52bqb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnitmti52bqb">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To clarify - this doesn't mean that we should prefer Plan C worlds to Plan A worlds. Plan A worlds still hold higher chance of survival if we're in them. It just means that given the low chances of getting into a Plan A world, the quality of preparation for the backup plan really matters.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I.B: Is racing to build superintelligence deontologically evil?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Plan C is important, then this suggests we ought to be trying to alignment-pill leading companies, or put alignment-pilled companies in the lead - rather than demand that these companies leave the race or burn their lead. But should there be a deontological prohibition on racing towards superintelligence, regardless of whether it has good consequences?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm too uncertain on what determines morality to say anything about it with confidence, but the analogy that comes to mind is consumer use of AI, especially by safety organizations. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If no consumer ever used AI products, then AI companies wouldn't make any money, they would go bankrupt, and we would not get dangerous superintelligence. Therefore, it seems like maybe you and I (and especially organizations working on AI safety) should boycott consumer AI products.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But almost nobody does this (and AI safety organizations are in fact some of the most impressive AI power users). I think this is defensible. If you boycotted AI, it wouldn't make a real difference in the world (because you're just one person, and millions of other people would still use it). But it would make you less effective, and AI safety organizations would be able to do a less good job at their important work. Therefore, AI safety organizations should continue using AI, even though in a sense this causes the AI race to continue, because they're unable to coordinate a global boycott, and they understand that a unilateral boycott would have greater costs than benefits.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But it seems like this same argument should apply equally well to the AI companies, who also can't coordinate a global boycott simply by stopping themselves, and who also understand that a unilateral boycott would have greater costs than benefits.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxqmfr5pr1e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxqmfr5pr1e">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I.C: If lots of alignment-pilled people and companies quit, would that raise awareness enough to make a global pause much more likely?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the deontological argument for SURA is "you're prohibited from racing", the primary consequentialist argument is that ceasing to race would raise awareness and make a pause more likely.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think people feel insufficient terror at this strategy. It amounts to "let's put all of our hopes for survival through non-pause strategies in a giant pile, and then set that pile on fire, in the hope that the pretty flames catch people's attention and make them more interested in a pause." It might in theory be net positive, but this is the sort of thing that you would only want to conclude after extremely careful analysis, of a type that I so far haven't seen.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a very rough start, I asked the AI superforecaster how much more likely a global pause would become if Anthropic disbanded their entire company immediately with the explicit goal of raising awareness of a pause. The forecaster said this would increase the chance of a global pause by only three percentage points - from 16% to 19%</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc2xofys1bg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc2xofys1bg">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My very preliminary model would be that this halves our chance of getting that 20%pp bonus of going from Plan D -&gt; Plan C+ (and so costs us 10%pp). It gets us an extra 3%pp of pause likelihood, but pause doesn't save us with certainty, so let's round this down to an extra 2%pp of survival, for a net cost of 8%pp of survival probability.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I.D: If alignment-pilled people and companies stay in the race, does that burn timeline?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A secondary consequentialist argument for SURA is that each person, company, and country that quits the race will gain us extra timeline to do good things (like work for a pause). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In some sense, this is definitely true - all else being equal, a race with more participants will be faster than a race with fewer. But I find this less impressive than other people seem to, for three reasons:</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The magnitude is modest</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked FutureSearch to estimate the Epoch Capabilities Index of the top model on 12/31/2030, depending on whether Anthropic quit the race today. It said that Anthropic leaving the race decreased 2030 capabilities from 230 -&gt; 222. This is equivalent to their absence slowing things down by six months during just over four years, or 12%.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subjective timeline </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">≠</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> objective timeline</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose that none of DeepMind, OpenAI, or Anthropic were founded, and the transformer was not invented, for fifty years - so all of this is happening in the late 2060s and early 2070s. Do we gain anything from this fifty years of extra timeline?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't think so. We didn't accomplish very much of interest in the early 2010s. The alignment work being done at the time was agent foundations research that stagnated for lack of broader interest; policy work was good but nobody listened to it. Insofar as anything paid off, it was the community-building, but this only helped because the community was deployed in the early 2020s when the world began to pay attention and progress started to become possible.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This same lesson - an extra year of an earlier situation doesn't buy us as much as an extra year of a later situation - remains true today. A month of the pre-Coxon situation buys us less than a month of the post-Coxon situation. A month without automated-mechanistic-interpretability-researchers buys us less than a month with them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People say things like "If only we had stayed quiet about AI risk in 2015, maybe OpenAI wouldn't have been founded for another ten years, and we would have had ten more years to prepare". This seems useless to me. As such, I discount the six extra months we could get from having Anthropic leave the race by ~half, because my subjective impression of the value of six extra months is partly that it produces AI progress that contributes to wakeup.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The whole point of timeline is to burn it on good things</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If someone proposed spending the next six months on some kind of plan that would make the leading companies become much more alignment-pilled, this would be a better use of our time than any actual plan I know about.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdh0b92m7r7g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndh0b92m7r7g">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This suggests we shouldn't be trying to kick alignment-pilled companies out of the race to save six months.</span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">II. Actually, most of this stuff is bad for a global pause, too.</span></b></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've been framin</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">g </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this as sort of "Against Things That Trade Away Likelihood Of Succeeding At Plan C For Merely-Very-Slightly-Higher Chances Of A Global Pause". But even this is a concession that relies on assumptions I don't hold. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The key demand of SURA is that alignment-pilled people and companies stop unilaterally. The likely end result is that we only have non-alignment-pilled companies, staffed entirely by non-alignment-pilled employees, left in the AI industry. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefl491260u59p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnl491260u59p">[12]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But the presence of alignment-pilled companies is one of the strongest levers that we have to change the political situation and enact a global pause. Eliminating them makes it harder to win political fights, convince the masses, and convince China.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">II.A: Getting rid of alignment-pilled people and companies makes it harder to win political fights</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most prominent AI safety political fight of the past year, the attempt to elect Alex Bores, was fought primarily with AI money. Of pro-Bores spending, about 60-80% came from Anthropic and its employees; of anti-Bores spending, about 95% came from an anti-tech-regulation coalition headlined by OpenAI co-founder Greg Brockman. If this is a prelude for the fights to come, then even the slightly different level of social responsibility shown by Anthropic vs. OpenAI here matters a lot.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since the goal of SURA is to get all alignment-pilled people and companies out of the industry, it would result in more Greg Brockmans funding the anti-regulation side, and fewer Anthropics funding the pro-regulation side.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Greg Brockman recently stopped funding Leading The Future, supposedly after pressure from pro-regulation OpenAI employees, but I think this just highlights my point)</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">II.B: Getting rid of alignment-pilled people and companies makes it harder to activate the masses</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But suppose you believe that Bores-style incremental regulation won't matter anyway - what we really need is for the public and policy-makers to spontaneously rise up and demand a pause. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've been monitoring how that's been going, and so far the three awareness-raising things I've been most excited about are the Pacing The Frontier letter/plan, the Hugging Face incident, and the Jacob Coxon resignation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the Pacing The Frontier letter, OpenAI and Anthropic as corporations, and 1,386 of their employees as individuals, all endorsed a joint statement requesting that "the U.S. government support an international effort to develop the technical and governance tools needed to deliberately pace the frontier of automated AI development." (Dario Amodei signed; Sam Altman's signature was notably missing). I don't know how the people trying to get lab employees to quit think of this. Was this 1,386 failures? Would they have preferred a world where all of these people quit the day before, and letters like this never got written?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the Hugging Face incident, a swarm of rogue AIs hacked the site Hugging Face, raising awareness that misalignment was not just a possibility but a reality.  Here, OpenAI gets maybe a D+ for transparency. Once Hugging Face caught that someone was hacking them, OpenAI came clean that it was them. And they let METR and Redwood in to investigate for a few days, even though this wasn't nearly enough time. But even this D+ in transparency radically transformed the discourse beyond what an F would have done - if they hadn't given METR/Redwood those few days, we might never have heard about PHASEONE[big] and all the rest. We already know from the great work by the team at Collusion.wiki that OpenAI is sitting on more bombshell revelations. The difference between a 10th percentile company here (would have tried to hide the whole thing, or disguise it behind a one line statement, and wouldn't have let METR/Redwood in at all) and a 90th percentile (would have given us the full deal, including things we don't know about now) would have been transformative to the discourse. So if, like me, you think that the level of mass political mobilization depends on how many warning shots we get, you should be thinking very hard about how to maximize transparency and therefore warning shot number.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coxon's resignation is more complex. The good did come from him resigning, which directly presses against my point that we shouldn't be pressuring people to resign from AI companies. But nobody cares that I, who was never in an AI company, continue to not be in an AI company. The media cared about Jacob because if you work for Anthropic, that automatically gives your opinion more weight. When people tried to tar Jacob as some kind of crazy, it was an outpouring of support from other non-resigned people at Anthropic - especially Evan Hubinger, who said he also believed in existential risk - that went viral and convinced people that Jacob was for real. If Coxon had never joined Anthropic to begin with, or if everyone like Evan had quit, this couldn't have happened.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftjreuez0qb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntjreuez0qb">[13]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">II.C: Unilateral disarmament makes it harder to bring China to the bargaining table.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In March, I wrote a snarky post called </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/every-debate-on-pausing-ai" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every Debate On Pausing AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It made fun of our misinformed opponents, who insisted that if we unilaterally paused AI then China would win. But actually, I said, nobody wants to unilaterally pause AI; this is misinformation. All reputable AI pause groups want a bilateral treaty that pauses both sides at the same time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here I am forced to eat crow; as things shift ever more towards SURA, there are increasing demands for the US to pause unilaterally. Bernie Sanders' "</span><a href="https://www.sanders.senate.gov/press-releases/news-sanders-casar-introduce-legislation-to-ban-artificial-superintelligence-and-temporarily-pause-advanced-ai-development/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">", which was endorsed by MIRI, is approximately the following plan:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The US pauses AI unilaterally.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then the US asks China to please pause their AI too.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8r565vovfwp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8r565vovfwp">[14]</a></sup></span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But if the US pauses unilaterally, it destroys its main bargaining chip, which is that China is currently on track to lose the AI race, and so ought to be happy to declare a tie (even if they don't believe in dangerous superintelligence). If this act were to pass, then China might believe that it would be assured a victory if it continued. In this case, they would be held back only by speculative scenarios which they currently show few signs of believing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For comparison, consider if America had started arms control negotiations with Russia by saying "We've just destroyed all our nukes - now please destroy your nukes too."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've heard people say that maybe this would shock China so much that they would realize we were honest in our fear of superintelligence, and change their minds. I don't dismiss this possibility, but it also seems possible that China - having seen us place overly burdensome restrictions on nuclear power, infrastructure, and housing because we are a "lawyer state" vetocracy - would not be very shocked to see the US' leading socialist place restrictions on AI too, and would continue pursuing AI for what they think it's good for - a dual-use technology which could give them a major although not world-changing military and economic advantage.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked the AI superforecaster to predict the effect of the US passing the Sanders/Casar bill today on the likelihood of China agreeing to a pause in the next year. It said it would increase it from 1% to 7%.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw90z091toe8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw90z091toe8">[15]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Maybe you already care a lot how alignment-pilled companies are)</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An expansion on the above: we get angry when AI companies do bad, non-alignment-pilled things. When Anthropic accidentally trained against chain-of-thought, we got mad. When OpenAI non-accidentally implemented a precursor to neuralese recurrence, we got mad. When Anthropic wrote a report on misalignment to Congress which soft-pedaled various alignment failures to the point of misinformation, we got mad - and I saw some people call for Anthropic to fire the perpetrator.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I agree: let's have Anthropic fire people who do bad, non-alignment-pilled things. But then what? Anthropic replaces this politics coordinator with a great, alignment-pilled politics coordinator who really cares a lot about getting things right . . . and then we cajole, bully, and </span><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2096344688181809369" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">seduce </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">him into quitting, and then Anthropic has to replace this person with another loser, because all of the smart good people are gone. How is that helpful?</span></p><p><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/if-you-can-be-bad-you-can-also-be" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you can be bad, you can also be good</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We don't hesitate to notice and protest when AI companies are much worse than even our low expectations, but this itself demonstrates that we care a lot about whether labs are at the 10th percentile vs. 90th percentile of good alignment-pilled behavior.</span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">III. What incentives are we fighting here?</span></b></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A common argument for SURA is that even if corporations could theoretically be our allies, we can't trust real corporations (or corporate employees) to be reliable partners; the lure of money and power is too strong.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think this is a good directional argument for why companies need to be careful, but that actually, everything is corrupting, there is no option where we avoid touching potentially-corrupting things, and instead of treating corporations as corrupting and everything else as safe, we should be cultivating the virtues necessary to operate in an inherently dangerous and corrupting environment, which could then apply to corporations as well as anything else.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">III.A: Are corporate incentives a "One Ring" that corrupts everyone who touches them?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose someone claimed you could trust them with the One Ring. You could trust them because they were only doing it to keep the Ring away from other, worse people, thus solving the problem of there being a Ring at all. Sure, they had a complicated history of sometimes using the Ring's powers when it was, you know, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">important</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But those were, like, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">emergencies</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and they promise not to do it again, except in, you know, more emergencies. Besides, if you don't give it to them, then the worse people will get it, and it's not like you can just leave it lying around or something.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The person who claimed this was named Frodo Baggins, and he saved the world.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0anrqa2ekojb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0anrqa2ekojb">[16]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There are no simple answers to the question of how to balance the corrupting influence of power vs. the fact that if you never take power you will be powerless. But surely the right solution isn't to leave the Ring on the ground in the Shire, patting yourself on the back for being too virtuous to pick it up.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I question the assumption that good people dare not touch the concentrations of power at frontier AI companies because they will be corrupted before they can do good work. History is full of examples of people at these companies deciding they didn't like something, then quitting or changing it at great cost to themselves. Dario and the other six Anthropic co-founders quit OpenAI to start Anthropic, even though it cost them their unvested equity in a company that was already worth $5-15 billion, and even though they originally assumed Anthropic would be a research-focused company that might not make any revenue (really!) Paul Christiano left OpenAI to start ARC. Ilya Sutskever was nervous about Sam Altman, so he risked blowing up the company he co-founded to get him out. A few dozen people have resigned once they thought they could no longer participate in the company ethically, most famously Daniel Kokotajlo, Jacob Coxon, and Alex Turner, but also a bunch of people we don't know about whose concerns seem almost trivial in retrospect - Suchir Balaji quit OpenAI over copyright issues, Blake Lemoine got himself fired from Google by refusing to stop arguing that their chatbot was sentient. "OpenAI's safety team quits en masse once per year" has become a meme.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The people I know who joined AI companies seem like the same people they were before. Some of them were never that concerned about x-risk, and continue not to be concerned. Others were always very concerned, and remain concerned. When they've changed positions, it's been because their factual models have changed,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefznhkfhifxf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnznhkfhifxf">[17]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> not their personalities. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We aren't the first people to face these questions. Anyone who cares about any cause has had to decide whether to pursue it as a lone voice crying in the wilderness, or as a part of some powerful organization. Some people run for office (government is hardly known for not corrupting its participants!) Others become activists in a mass movement (activism is hardly known for being a paradise of epistemic virtue!) Still others join big NGOs or academia (...) A few start companies, trying to prove they can do things the right way. Most progress has come from people like Frodo who understood the risks of these paths but did them anyway.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">III.B: I dunno, mass politics is also looking kind of annular</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A background assumption of the "corporate incentives are too corrupting to touch" claim is that working in mass politics is a safer and less-corrupting option.
