Given famous LessWrong posts like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/politics-is-the-mind-killer" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Politics Is The Mind-Killer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I think we should be more skeptical of this assumption.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The climate movement is the natural control group for the AI safety movement. Some prescient scientists realized that global warming was happening and could get worse. They spent a while raising awareness. As the movement grew, its epistemics worsened. It turned into a circular firing squad, where different activist groups competed to accuse one another of being insufficiently edgy and radical. By the end of this process, many climate activists were fighting </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">against </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">everything from cap-and-trade to carbon capture to nuclear power, claiming they were "techno-solutions" that distracted from the root problem (not always named, but uncharitably something like capitalism or progress). The only acceptable strategy was to keep raising awareness to get stronger government regulation, without much interest in whether other countries followed suit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This has some resemblance to SURA, and I think this is more than coincidental. Convergent evolution is trying to carcinize us into yet another political-movement-shaped crab. SURA-as-politics is turning into a </span><a href="https://squid314.livejournal.com/329171.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"superweapon"</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, bristling with its own lexicon that makes dissent hard to express:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People who express high pdooms get lauded as "frank" and "courageous"; people who express lower ones get condemned for "deferring" or "downplaying".</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Any strategy besides SURA, or any attempt to consider race dynamics, gets called "galaxy-brained".</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All strategies beyond SURA get lumped together as "EA". Movement history is relitigated to make "EA" the source of all bad things. Since SURA is the best strategy and the public is easy to convince, why didn't we do it years ago and win easily?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnfosab9exvf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnfosab9exvf">[18]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It's not because this is hard, or because race dynamics are real - it's because "EA" stood in the way and banned it.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/arguments-as-soldiers?version=1.11.0" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arguments are now soldiers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. "BuT tHe RaCe WiTh ChInA!!!" is the sort of thing an accelerationist would say. Are you an accelerationist? Then why are you bringing up the race with China? I have had some very scary (to me) conversations where somebody new to the movement tries to make conversation with me about how pathetic it is that companies sometimes use "the race with China" as an excuse to keep operating. When I ask them why they think this argument fails, they get confused and flustered to see somebody they previously respected take "the race with China" seriously as a real thing. I remember one case in particular where they then freaked out, realizing that in fact we really were in a race with China, nobody had any plan for it, and this thing that they had previously pooh-poohed as an outgroup slogan was actually the linchpin of the whole situation.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because arguments are soldiers, people feel licensed to retaliate against anyone who believes the "wrong" arguments . When I posted an earlier (and admittedly less-polished, and more vehement) version of these concerns to social media, I had an organization that had previously given me lots of free help with Astral Codex Ten state that they would now be charging me money (albeit still at a 90% discount) because they thought I might be causing harm. Although they're under no obligation to give me free labor, and I'm grateful that they ever did, I do think this is a warning sign that conditions are ripe for </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Tw9cLvzSKrkGjNHW3/when-none-dare-urge-restraint" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">preference cascades</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZQG9cwKbct2LtmL3p/evaporative-cooling-of-group-beliefs" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">evaporative cooling</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of this produces epistemic closure - if someone disagrees with SURA, or worries too much about race dynamics, then they've either been corrupted by the companies, or are "deferring to" "EA".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It looks like what one would expect if mass politics were trying to taking AI safety on the same path as climate activism or socialism. The clearest analogy is to false consciousness - the socialists' idea that everyone who doesn't currently agree with them has either been corrupted by their proximity to money/power, or at least brainwashed by a hegemonic capitalist system.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793231138/lexical_client_uploads/pib79oh4ket3yig4nn06.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See also </span></i><a href="https://geesemag.com/articles/psls-top-propagandist-resigns-publishes-tell-all" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this article in a socialist magazine</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about how every tiny socialist party believes that if they could only knock out their true enemies - all the other tiny socialist parties - then they could get a monopoly on socialism and overthrowing capitalism would be easy. Cf. SURA as the belief that if we could only knock out our true enemy - Anthropic, effective altruists, etc, then defeating misaligned AI would happen naturally. For example, why do we criticize Anthropic more than every other AI company combined?</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Exhibit 1: Sigal Samuel's  "Anthropic Could Just Stop")</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most recently, I've seen people in the AI safety community reblogging and pointing to Sigal Samuel's article </span><a href="https://archive.is/GeZ0t" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic Could Just Stop</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (seen elsewhere under the title "Anthropic Should Just Walk Away From The AI Race"). I think it's a good example of what I'm complaining about - something which uses the right shibboleths, makes it </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sound</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> like race dynamics have been solved and we don't have to worry about them - but ultimately has no answer to the race dynamics which are the whole point of worrying about this question.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some sample phrases from the article:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"In fact, part of the problem may be that Anthropic continues to treat this as a classic arms race...but what if this framing misses the point?"</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Game theory can make a huge gamble seem sophisticated, while making the obvious alternative — just don’t build a doom machine — seem naive."</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Yet the fact that Anthropic and others are now racing toward RSI makes it much more likely that we could lose control of AI and start to see massive damage to our world."</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"When the cost of defecting from safety is that you create a serious risk of catastrophic harm — or of all humanity, including you, going extinct — is that still a “rational” move?"</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Instead of telling yourself that you can galaxy-brain your way through this with fancy enough game theory, maybe you’re better off just thinking of this as something much simpler: Russian roulette."</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"AI insiders often think those outside of their industry can’t possibly understand the technical, geopolitical, and moral complexities they’re facing. But commonsense morality is commonsense for a reason"</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"If you don’t want a doom machine, maybe don’t build a doom machine."</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Believing you’re smart enough to take highly counterintuitive actions and end up saving the world as a result is an extremely risky gambit. Instead, maybe it’s best to just do the straightforwardly right thing: Stop."</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This hits all the points I'm complaining about. Although it uses big words, its basic thesis is  anti-intellectual - all of these "smart" people claiming to be "rational" with their "game theory" are missing the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">common-sense</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> point which is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">obvious</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to all</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> normal</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> people - just stop making "galaxy-brained" excuses and "just don't build a doom machine".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But where is its actual argument that race dynamics don't matter? Or that Anthropic leaving the race won't end up with OpenAI / Meta / China continuing to race and creating an unaligned superintelligence? The only place I can find where Samuel attempts to address the possibility is this section:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s a perennial objection here: “But China!”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yet the idea that we’re in a classic arms race with China </span><a href="https://archive.is/o/GeZ0t/https://www.vox.com/the-highlight/23621198/artificial-intelligence-chatgpt-openai-existential-risk-china-ai-safety-technology" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">has always been flawed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For starters, AI is not just one thing with one purpose, like the atomic bomb. It’s a much more general-purpose technology, like electricity. So what matters isn’t simply who gets it first, but who uses it in which ways. Besides, if the harms from AI are massive, then those harms are relevant for the other party, too. The Chinese don’t want to see catastrophic harms to all of human civilization, either. So we shouldn’t write off the possibility of coordination.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Former Vox reporter </span><a href="https://archive.is/o/GeZ0t/https://www.theargumentmag.com/p/what-i-saw-inside-anthropic" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kelsey Piper recently wrote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about a colloquium at Anthropic where one participant objected: A decade ago, Facebook considered acquiring the tech that would later power TikTok. But, facing pressure due to privacy and safety worries, the company passed. That didn’t end up protecting us from the scourges of a world with TikTok — all it did was hand China an early monopoly on the tech.  . . . But TikTok is not a good analogy for RSI. The former may be a cesspool that rots our brains and amplifies ethnic hatred, but its dangers don’t compare to the dangers of RSI. The TikTok case may indeed be best approached as a classic prisoner’s dilemma. But with RSI, the potential downside is so catastrophic that anyone who proposes to save the world precisely by building the tech that endangers it is making a hell of a gamble on behalf of everyone else. At the very least, the burden of proof should be on them to prove their confidence is justified.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It starts by framing this in a maximally hostile way ("a perennial objection...'but China!'), letting us know we should dismiss it as an enemy soldier. Then it says that "AI is not just one thing with one purpose...so what matters isn't simply who gets it first, but who uses it in which ways". I don't understand how this is supposed to relate to the worry that China will remain in the race if Anthropic leaves, especially the fear that nobody will be able to "use it" at all because it will be misaligned.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Next, it says that since the harms from AI are massive, everyone except Anthropic might just stop on their own if Anthropic stops first. Big if true! </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(again, the AI superforecaster assigns this a 3% chance)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is just hand-waving away the potential that there might still be race dynamics after all the good guys drop out. But the whole point of the good guys not immediately dropping out is the belief that the race dynamics will continue. Indeed, the race dynamics continuing ought to be the very-high-prior default hypothesis, since everyone else has said they're not going to drop out, and since other companies dropping out seems strictly less likely than Anthropic doing so.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpucr8agdmti"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpucr8agdmti">[19]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> As such, even though this article is very long and detailed, it feels like a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SwYBLQvo8MddDcCwz/cat-belling-problems" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cat-belling problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where it addresses every objection except the central one that was the only reason anyone ever disagreed in the first place.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't mean to attack Samuel too hard here. She is faithfully describing the position as best she can; I think the position itself struggles to answer this question.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(A virtuous exception)</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I want to thank the one group I've heard advocate for an immediate unilateral-ish pause in a way that makes sense and considers race dynamics carefully.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI Futures Project </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/how-to-pace-the-us-frontier" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">currently believes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that it might be correct for AI companies to pause immediately, though it's unclear whether they're thinking of a single company, or US companies as a group.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Their argument is that Plan C requires companies to do some combination of race (at some points) and burn their lead (at other points).</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They think US companies shouldn't race now, because their security is so bad that China will steal whatever progress they make, and they won't get any extra lead anyway. Therefore, companies should pause now, spend the time on alignment and tightening their security. Then, if nobody has figured out a global pause by the time the companies get their security in order, they can go back to racing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This makes sense, and I exempt it from my criticism.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IV. What if we survive?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't think this should be our main consideration - we should start with trying to minimize the odds of extinction - but I still think it's important.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we survive x-risk, the next battle is concentration-of-power risks. It may be that one group, or a tiny number of groups, ends up with all the wealth and power in the world. They'll face a choice between possible actions including:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keep the wealth/power; become a benevolent dictator.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keep the wealth/power, become dictator, don't worry too much about the benevolence.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hand the wealth/power over to the US government in a poorly-planned way that results in dictatorship or oligarchy.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hand the wealth/power over to the US government in a well-planned way that preserves democracy.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hand the wealth/power over to the Chinese government.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hand the wealth/power to the people of their country directly.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hand the wealth/power over to the people of the world directly.</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hand the wealth/power over to the people of the world, plus some mechanism for protecting the interests of non-people like animals and conscious AIs.</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hand the wealth/power over to a sovereign AI with some sort of complicated utility function.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's all nice and well to say that we should never let ourselves get in a position where anyone has all of these options, and that national governments should form a plan for what to do before this - not even the real national government led by its current leaders, but some ideal corruption-free national government which represents all of our best impulses. But, much like a global pause, this is a great outcome which we might not be able to prepare in time. If we get ASI next year, the ideal perfect government might be nowhere in sight; we might face a situation in which Trump demands absolute power, and the leading AI company has to decide whether to give it to him. Or one where either the USA or China will get control of the entire future, and which one it is depends on which AI company is in the lead. Or one where democracy miraculously survives, but the AI company and its investors end up with 90% of all wealth, and they have to decide whether to redistribute it vs. fight to keep it (using superintelligent lobbyists that give them significant control over the democratic government.) If any of this happens, the shape of the future might depend on who the leading AI companies are.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a certain Far Mode sense in which believing that Dario Amodei and Demis Hassabis are exactly as untrustworthy as Elon Musk and Xi Jinping helps reminds us of universal human fallibility. But do we want to bet the world on it being literally true?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think that a significant percent of people, put in a position of absolute power, would try to do something good, like make sure AI follows some kind of democratic rule of law. And some other significant percent of people would try to do something bad, like become a dictator. Saying "we should never let anyone get into this situation" isn't a solution, it's a wish - it may not come true, and we shouldn't be pursuing plans that will fail catastrophically if it doesn't.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SURA effectively removes all ethical people from the bargaining table in situations like these, ensuring that - unless the virtuous government representing our best impulses really does appear and takes control of the situation - the future of humanity will be handed over to sociopaths and cowards.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">V. What's The Alternative To SURA?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The alternative to SURA isn't abandoning awareness-raising, mass activism, and support for a global pause. All of these strategies are great and important. It's viewing them as part of a portfolio of different options, none of which need to be set on fire to boost the others.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead of spending our corporate activism energy telling companies to stop unilaterally, I think we should spend it:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">first, trying to get companies to agree to stop in a coordinated manner</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">second, if that doesn't work, trying to get companies to prepare for Plan C+/C</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">V.A: Getting companies to agree to stop in a coordinated manner</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By stop in a coordinated manner, I mean get them to sign a commitment saying that if every other major company in every country agrees to stop or slow by some definition, they'll also stop or slow by that definition.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is strictly easier than getting them to stop unilaterally. And, unlike stopping unilaterally, it fails gracefully in a situation where some companies agree to it but not others.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was the original goal of the "Stop The AI Race" protests. It doesn't seem to have worked well, because most companies backed a vague statement agreeing with this on vibes (the Pacing the Frontier letter), and then nothing happened. Also, I think SURA got to the STAIR people too, and now they're demanding labs pause unilaterally instead.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We should be trying to figure out what a binding version of this commitment would look like. Additionally, we should be spending more energy figuring out what the antitrust barriers to such a statement are, how to get around them, and what demands we want to make for companies to violate antitrust law (it seems plausible that we should demand they do this and let the administration pay the PR cost of trying to stop them, but this is an unconsidered opinion and it might be that there are better options).</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(...and then we know where we stand)</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If all American, but no Chinese companies are willing to sign the commitment, then we know that we have a diplomatic problem, and can concentrate on diplomacy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If all Chinese, but no American companies are willing to sign the commitment, then we know that we have a domestic problem, and can concentrate on domestic legislation, encouraging policymakers by letting them know that if they force US companies to comply, there are no further concerns about international race dynamics.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If everyone except one company is willing to sign the commitment, then we can start putting pressure on that one company, boycotting their products, and applying as much social sanction as we can to their executives and employees.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If all leading companies, but no trailing companies, are willing to sign the commitment, and we can't change this, then we know that we're in the right position to focus on Plan C+ or Plan C.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">V.B: Getting companies to prepare for Plan C+/Plan C if that doesn't work</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Although we should hope for the best, we should assume that not all companies will sign a conditional pause commitment, and prepare for what to do.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In my modal non-hopeless play-to-our-outs world, Anthropic, Google, and OpenAI are all willing to sign this commitment, but Meta/X/China aren't. If this happens, the good coalition has a ~six-month lead over the bad coalition. Plan C tells us to have them join together and mutually burn their lead on alignment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All three companies have a self-image as the sort of people who would do this if it came to that, but in reality they are likely to get distracted or get into fights or delay until it's too late. We should be focusing on changing that.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We should get companies to once again consider legally binding weak merge-and-assist clauses. These could say that if a certain authority says we're less than X months to a certain type of dangerous superintelligence, they will jointly stop training and focus on alignment, while sharing all of their alignment secrets.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We should boost company security, so that trailing actors can't easily steal leading actors' weights and eliminate their lead.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We should be preparing to loop the best rationalist/EA technical alignment organizations into the coalition of good companies, so they can join in the alignment spring / lead-burning effort.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In one sense, all of this is strictly easier than getting companies to quit unilaterally. It fits with the role these companies like to imagine themselves having. Everyone who previously hoped to win can at least hope to tie for a win. There is low downside risk of accidentally getting superintelligence built by someone who fails to even try our best alignment techniques, or handing over the future to bad actors.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The only sense in which it's harder is that it's a worse fit for (a very pessimistic view of) mass politics, where voters are too simple to consider any worldview more subtle than AI bad = AI companies bad = we should protest AI companies until they stop. I admit that "make AI companies prepare for Plan C" sounds worse at a protest than "demand that AI companies stop unilaterally".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you believe that mass politics is unable to promote any ideas except maximally-simple, potentially-counterproductive ones, I think this is an argument against hinging a strategy on mass politics.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0ffafiyvy71f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0ffafiyvy71f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post isn't affiliated with AIFP and I think they disagree with some of it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5uxe04oh537"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5uxe04oh537">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan C is for the leading company to burn its lead. Plan C+ is similar, but all the companies in the leading country work together (maybe because the government is forcing them). Plan B is similar, but the leading country is also sabotaging trailing countries. In Plan D, there is no leading company, and companies can only do alignment work sporadically when they get the chance.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnemu1umykit"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefemu1umykit">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If alignment-pilled companies aren't in the lead, then we end up with the worst version of Plan D, where we have to hope that incidental alignment work done along the way by commercially-focused companies proves to be enough</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn650o2ryrvd3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref650o2ryrvd3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem with China getting superintelligence first isn't just that they might misuse it. It's that, on Plan C, US companies are working hard to align AI; if Chinese companies aren't doing this, then the chance that the first superintelligence is aligned goes down.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn10iyj7shymno"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref10iyj7shymno">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't want to get in a fight over whether more good or bad alignment surprises have happened recently, but I also updated on </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/mysteries-of-ai-generalization"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/mysteries-of-ai-generalization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://x.com/_jake_ward/status/2112264244767211854"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://x.com/_jake_ward/status/2112264244767211854</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - not as solutions in and of themselves, but as evidence that good things can happen that I wasn't previously considering. Nick Cammarata's </span><a href="https://already-clean.goodfire.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neural Networks Are Already Clean</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> would also be in this category if I believed it, but I'm currently confused by the debate around it and unsure.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fna4fo9t0jai"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefa4fo9t0jai">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many people at eg MIRI have thought about this in much greater depth than I have and concluded, on the basis of a gears-level model, that near-term alignment is very unlikely to work. I'm still trying to understand their arguments, but I don't blame them for having them. But I do worry that other people have picked up SURA ideas from the MIRI-sphere without realizing that they make less sense without MIRI's particular brand of strong alignment skepticism.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6wx02b71g4t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6wx02b71g4t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, this is just the people who have formally considered this question qua forecasters and written down exact numbers. I'm not denying that there are many other smart people, for example at MIRI, who have thought about this hard and believe the opposite.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnitmti52bqb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefitmti52bqb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, despite leaning heavily on AIFP terminology and sources in this essay, I'm not claiming that AIFP or its members agree with my thesis in this post, and Daniel Kokotajlo in particular has said pretty clearly that he hopes people unilaterally quit AI companies.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxqmfr5pr1e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxqmfr5pr1e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I discuss this further at </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/what-deontological-bars" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What Deontological Bars?</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc2xofys1bg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc2xofys1bg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a higher probability than it gives for global pauses in other situations, I think because it unintentionally magnifies small differences in how I frame the question into different definitions of "global pause". I haven't put in enough work to investigate and have a strong opinion on what's going on; don't take these numbers too seriously except as a very rough order of magnitude guess.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndh0b92m7r7g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdh0b92m7r7g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm working on a grantmaking AI that can actually compare the values of different outcomes. It's still in alpha, and to some degree this is a circular argument because I'm trying to get it to mimic/predict my own opinions, but very loosely, it thinks that increasing the alignment-pilled-ness of the leading company from OpenAI-level to Anthropic-level would be worth about 250x as much as the best (low-enough-secrecy-to-mention-here) actual grant proposal it's ever evaluated, which was a proposed $2 million to the AI Futures Project. I think this confirms my "better use of our time than any actual plan we can carry out in those six months" claim.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnl491260u59p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefl491260u59p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I'm modeling this, including on the AI grantmaker, I'm imagining a scale from 1 - 100, where NVIDIA is 10, Meta or X is about 25, OpenAI is about 50, GDM is about 60, and Anthropic is about 80. I imagine a successful campaign to get alignment-pilled people to quit taking Anthropic out entirely, and reducing GDM/OAI to about 40 as they lose their alignment-pilled employees and executives.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntjreuez0qb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftjreuez0qb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I remember how much of a slog the discourse was back in 2015, when the standard counterargument was "you're just a bunch of cultists, no real AI experts believe in this doom stuff". The new standard counterargument of "Of course all the AI experts believe in this, they're just hyping their business for the IPO" is actually a vast improvement!</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8r565vovfwp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8r565vovfwp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked a supporter how they saw this working - they said that if the US paused, and China didn't seem on track to follow suit, maybe the US would start up again after a few months. But A) this would burn somewhere between most and all of the lead, B) this was just their speculation, and there's nothing in the bill which suggests that the US would do this.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw90z091toe8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw90z091toe8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, all of these pause numbers are slightly different because the question is slightly different  - this one is about the next year only. But I want to get people thinking in probabilities and present some evidence that I'm not letting my own biases control me, and head off accusations that I'm just trying to spread fear and pessimism. I don't know what is the AI equivalent of Gollum.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnznhkfhifxf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefznhkfhifxf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Specifically, there are many people who said they wouldn't try to make money or release products who changed their tune when they realized that AI would require hundreds of billions of dollars of compute, and that the only way to get that money was to get hyperscaler and investor support.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnfosab9exvf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnfosab9exvf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One reason all of this confuses me is that rationalists had an excellent model of race dynamics with China as early as the mid-2010s, when we understood they would be an inevitable part of any future AI development. These discussions included plenty of talk about whether we would be able to coordinate a global pause, but I don't remember anyone ever seriously saying that the problem could be solved if the good alignment-pilled companies or countries dropped out unilaterally.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpucr8agdmti"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpucr8agdmti">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the many ways I have trouble modeling why Samuel would write this article is that it seems like she trusts everyone to drop out of their own accord, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">except for</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Anthropic.
